Taylor expansions of Jacobi forms and linear relations among theta series
Xiao-Jie Zhu

Abstract
. We study Taylor expansions of Jacobi forms of lattice index. As the main result, we give an embedding from certain space of such forms, whether scalar-valued or vector-valued, integral-weight or half-integral-weight, of any level, with any character, into a product of finitely many spaces of modular forms. As an application, we investigate linear relations among Jacobi theta series of lattice index. Many linear relations among the second powers of such theta series associated with the D 4 D_{4} lattice and A 3 A_{3} lattice are obtained, along with relations among the third powers of series associated with the A 2 A_{2} lattice. We present the complete SageMath code for the D 4 D_{4} lattice.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Taylor expansions of Jacobi forms

The concept of Jacobi forms of lattice index, or equivalently, Jacobi forms on
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathbb{C}^{\mathfrak{n}}
(
\mathfrak{H}
denotes the upper half plane), was first investigated by Gritsenko
[
Gri88
]
, where the author studied their relationship to modular forms on the orthogonal group of a quadratic space of signature
(2,\mathfrak{n})
, and the action of Hecke operators on them. This generalized the theory of classical Jacobi forms developed by Eichler and Zagier
[
EZ85
]
. A further generalization to Siegel-Jacobi forms on
\mathfrak{H}_{j}\times\mathbb{C}^{j\times\mathfrak{n}}
was obtained by Ziegler
[
Zie89
]
in the spirit of Eichler and Zagier. The present paper focuses on Jacobi forms of lattice index, so by the term “Jacobi forms” we mean forms of lattice index. Ajouz, under supervision of Skoruppa and Walling, developed systematically the Hecke theory of such forms, and the relation to elliptic modular forms, in his doctoral thesis
[
Ajo15
]
.

Ajouz investigated liftings to and from elliptic modular forms via many tools, such as a generalization of Skoruppa-Zagier liftings
[
SZ88
]
, Shimura correspondence (
[
Shi73
]
,
[
Niw75
]
and
[
Cip83
]
), stable isomorphisms between lattices (
[
Nik79
]
), theta decompositions and Weil representations (
[
Ajo15
, §2.3, §2.4]
). On the contrary, in the present paper, we shall investigate the relation between Jacobi forms and elliptic modular forms via Taylor expansions with respect to the point
0\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathfrak{n}}
. In this direction, there are many excellent works. Eichler and Zagier has worked out the scalar index case. Ibukiyama
[
Ibu12
]
investigated Taylor expansions of Jacobi forms on
\mathfrak{H}_{j}\times\mathbb{C}^{j\times 1}
. Bringmann, Mahlburg, and Rhoades
[
BMR14
]
considered those of mock-Jacobi forms. Later Bringmann
[
Bri18
]
considered those of non-holomorphic Jacobi forms. More recently, Ittersum
[
vI21
]
, motivated by the problem of finding complex-valued functions on all partitions that are quasimodular forms for congruence subgroups, developed a theory of Taylor expansions of strictly meromorphic quasi-Jacobi forms.

We describe briefly our main result (Theorem
6.2
). For any weight (integral or half-integral), any integral lattice (even or odd), any finite index subgroup of the modular group, and any representation with a finite index kernel, we obtain embeddings of the space of Jacobi forms (vector-valued or scalar-valued) into the direct product of finite number of spaces of elliptic modular forms. A key point is how to choose these spaces of modular forms, for which we give a computational criterion (Proposition
6.9
, realized as a SageMath program
[
Sag21
]
).

To avoid overlapping the main text, we illustrate the main theorem with a typical example (scalar-valued and integral-weight). Let
L=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, equipped with a quadratic form
1
1

1

The reason why we choose such a “strange” quadratic form is that for more familiar form of determinant
3
, such as
x^{2}-xy+y^{2}
, we need at least five spaces of modular forms to construct an embedding to the best of our ability, while for the form presented here, we need only three spaces.

Q(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+xy+2y^{2}
. The bilinear form associated with this quadratic form is
B((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))=Q(x_{1}+x_{2},y_{1}+y_{2})-Q(x_{1},y_{1})-Q(x_{2},y_{2})
. The Gram matrix is
G_{\mathfrak{B}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{1}\\
{1}&{4}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, so
Q(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}(x,y)G_{\mathfrak{B}}(x,y)^{T}
. Put
\underline{L}=(L,B)
. Let
k
be a positive integer,
G
be a finite index subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, and
\chi\colon G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a group character whose kernel is of finite index. The space
J_{k,\underline{L}}(G,\chi)
, by definition, consists of all holomorphic functions
\phi(\tau,z)
on
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
subject to the following conditions:

(1)

For any
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in G
,

\phi\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\frac{z}{c\tau+d}\right)=(c\tau+d)^{k}\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{cQ(z)}{c\tau+d}\right)\chi\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\phi(\tau),

(2)

For any
v,w\in L
,

\phi\left(\tau,z+\tau v+w\right)=\mathfrak{e}\left(-\tau Q(v)-B(v,z)\right)\phi(\tau,z),

(3)

For any
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, we have

(c\tau+d)^{-k}\mathfrak{e}\left(-\frac{cQ(z)}{c\tau+d}\right)\phi\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\frac{z}{c\tau+d}\right)\\
=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{n\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0},\,t\in L^{\sharp}}\\
{Q(t)\leq n}\end{subarray}}c(n,t)\mathfrak{e}\left(n\tau+B(t,z)\right),

where
c(n,t)\in\mathbb{C}
, and the series converges absolutely.

The notation
\mathfrak{e}\left(x\right)
means
\exp(2\uppi\mathrm{i}x)
, and
L^{\sharp}
denotes the dual lattice of
L
. The reader may also compare this definition to
[
GSZ19
, §9, Definition]
, and should notice a subtle difference: there is no factor
\mathfrak{e}\left(Q(v+w)\right)
in the second condition in our definition, but there is such one in
[
GSZ19
]
. (As a side effect, in
[
GSZ19
]
, the variable
t
in
c(n,t)
ranges over
L_{ev}^{\sharp}
, not
L^{\sharp}
.) Both definitions make sense, but they correspond to different spaces! For the definition in broad generalities, including the case of half-integral weights, of even or odd lattices, and of scalar-valued or vector-valued forms, see Definition
2.3
and
2.6
. Put
q^{n}=\mathfrak{e}\left(n\tau\right)
for tradition. Let
M_{k}(G,\chi)
and
S_{k}(G,\chi)
denote the space of modular forms, and that of cusp forms of weight
k
on the group
G
with character
\chi
respectively. Then our main result, for this special case, says that:

Theorem

(A special case of Corollary
6.7
)
.

The map

\displaystyle J_{k,\underline{L}}(G,\chi)

\displaystyle\rightarrow M_{k}(G,\chi)\times S_{k+1}(G,\chi)\times S_{k+2}(G,\chi)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto(D_{0}(\phi),D_{1}(\phi),D_{2}(\phi))

is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear embedding, where

\displaystyle\phi(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,t}c(n,t)q^{n}\mathfrak{e}\left(B(t,z)\right),

\displaystyle D_{0}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\left(\sum_{t}c(n,t)\right)q^{n},

\displaystyle D_{1}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\left(\sum_{t}c(n,t)\cdot(t_{1}+4t_{2})\right)q^{n},

\displaystyle D_{2}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\left(\sum_{t}c(n,t)\cdot\left(k(t_{1}+4t_{2})^{2}-4n\right)\right)q^{n}.

It is proper in this place to explain the relation of “Taylor expansions” in the title and the main theorem: each coefficient of terms of
D_{0}(\phi)
,
D_{1}(\phi)
,
D_{2}(\phi)
is some modified Taylor coefficient of
\phi
.

We develop necessary tools to prove the main theorem from Section
2
to
5
. In Section
2
, we recall basic definitions and properties of Jacobi forms in broad generalities. In Section
3
, we develop some operators used to define the sequence of operators
D_{0}(\phi)
,
D_{1}(\phi)
and so on. To get rid of the problem of convergence, we work with formal Laurent series of several variables which transform like modular forms. At the end of this section, we obtain operators that map formal Laurent series of low weights to series of high weights. See Proposition
3.21
for details. Note that results in this section may be of independent interest. In Section
4
, we describe operators that map Jacobi forms to modular forms. It turns out that for each vector
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\mathfrak{n}}
, where
\mathfrak{n}
is the number of elliptic variables, there is an operator
D_{k,\mathbf{p}}
that maps Jacobi forms of weight
k
to elliptic modular forms of weight
k+s(\mathbf{p})
, where
s(\mathbf{p})
is the sum of
\mathbf{p}
’s components. See Theorem
4.6
for details. As would be discussed in Remark
4.7
, this theorem belongs to van Ittersum. We reprove it in a different approach. Then we give the
q
-expansion of each operator
D_{k,\mathbf{p}}
acting on Jacobi forms (Proposition
4.11
), which plays an important role in the following. At the end of this section (Proposition
4.15
), we prove the direct product of all these operators, gives an isomorphism from all formal Laurent series which transform like modular forms, onto the direct product of infinitely many spaces of modular forms, hence gives an embedding of the space of Jacobi forms, into the same codomain. This proof is in the spirit of Eichler and Zagier (compare it to
[
EZ85
, p. 34]
). Now for proving the main theorem, it remains to show how to choose finitely many operators such that the direct product is still injective, for which we need the technique of theta decompositions. Thus, in Section
5
we review the theory of Jacobi theta series of lattice index, Weil representations and theta decompositions. We prove a version of theta decompositions (Theorem
5.2
) which serves our purpose. The rest of this section is devoted to discussing the relation between theta decompositions and Taylor expansions. After these preparations we state and prove the main theorem in Section
6
. The finitely many operators
D_{k,\mathbf{p}}
are choosen in such way that in the theta decomposition of any Jacobi form
\phi
,
D_{k,\mathbf{p}}\phi=0
for all these
\mathbf{p}
could imply the theta coefficients of
\phi
all vanish, and hence
\phi
itself vanishes. The rest of this section contains some immediate corollaries dealing with lattices of determinant
2
and
3
, and a computational criterion (Proposition
6.9
) for deciding what
\mathbf{p}
’s to choose.

1.2.
Linear relations among theta series

In a systematic treatment of “cubic theta functions”
[
Sch13
]
, Schultz defined, among others, nine theta functions, related to
\sum q^{m^{2}+mn+n^{2}}\zeta_{1}^{m}\zeta_{2}^{n}
, and studied their inversion formulas, addition formulas and modular equations. See
[
Sch13
, §2.3]
for the precise definition of these nine functions. He proved some linear relations among the third powers of these functions (
[
Sch13
, Corollary 4.3]
), which generalize
[
BB91
, Equation (2.3)]
. These nine functions, can be rewritten as Jacobi theta series of lattice index associated with the
A_{2}
lattice as follows:

\theta_{\alpha,\beta}(\tau,z)=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathfrak{e}\left(B(\beta,v)\right)\mathfrak{e}\left(\tau Q(\alpha+v)+B(\alpha+v,z)\right),

where
(\tau,z)\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
B(x,y)=x\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{2}&{-1}\\
{-1}&{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot y^{T}
, and
Q(x)=\frac{1}{2}B(x,x)
for
x,y\in\mathbb{C}^{1\times 2}
. Then Schultz’s identities are equivalent to

Theorem
.

Let
\alpha
be one of the followings:
(0,0),\,(2/3,1/3),\,(1/3,2/3)
, and
\beta
be one of the followings:
(0,0),\,(1/3,1/3),\,(2/3,2/3)
. We have

\theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{3}+\theta_{0,0}^{3}=\theta_{\alpha,0}^{3}+\theta_{0,\beta}^{3}.

As an application of the main theorem (Theorem
6.2
), we give an algorithm for searching and proving this type of identities, not only for the
A_{2}
lattice, but for arbitrary lattice in principle. More precisely, for any even integral positive definite symmetric bilinear form
B
on
L=\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
(extended to
\mathbb{C}^{\mathfrak{n}}
by bilinearity), and suitably choosen
\alpha
and
\beta
, we could find all linear relations among functions
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{N^{\prime}}
, where
N^{\prime}
is a fixed positive integer dividing the level of
(L,B)
(the least positive integer
N
such that
N\cdot Q(x)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
). The algorithm roughly says the following.

Theorem

(A rough version of Theorem
7.10
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}
be the set of all pairs
(\mathbf{p},n)
with
\mathbf{p}
ranging over a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\mathfrak{n}}
such that the product
\prod D_{N^{\prime}\mathfrak{n}/2,\mathbf{p}}
is injective, and

n=0,\,1,\,2,\dots,\left[\frac{\delta_{N}N^{2}}{24}\left(\frac{N^{\prime}\mathfrak{n}}{2}+s(\mathbf{p})\right)\prod_{p\mid N}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\right].

For any
(\alpha,\beta)\in L^{\sharp}/L\times(L^{\sharp}+\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}L)/L^{\sharp}
with
Q(\alpha)\in\frac{1}{N^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}
, we associate the theta series
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{N^{\prime}}
with an
\mathfrak{I}
-indexed sequence
\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}\colon\mathfrak{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
whose
(\mathbf{p},n)
-term is equal to

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{v_{1},\dots,v_{N^{\prime}}\in L}\\
{Q(\alpha+v_{1})+\dots+Q(\alpha+v_{N^{\prime}})=n}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{e}\left(B(\beta,v_{1}+\dots+v_{N^{\prime}})\right)\\
\times P_{\mathbf{p}}\left(n,\frac{(\alpha+v_{1})+\dots(\alpha+v_{N^{\prime}})}{N^{\prime}}\right),

where
P_{\mathbf{p}}
is certain
(\mathfrak{n}+1)
-ary polynomial. Then any linear relation among
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{N^{\prime}}
’s holds if and only if the linear relation with the same coefficients among
\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}
’s holds.

For the precise version (and definitions of concepts not introduced yet), see Theorem
7.10
. For the definition of the polynomial
P_{\mathbf{p}}
, see Definition
4.10
.

To find linear relations among
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{N^{\prime}}
, we need only calculate the vectors
\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}
, and then produce linear relations among them using standard linear algebra algorithm. We use SageMath
[
Sag21
]
to do all computations. Besides Schultz’s identities for the
A_{2}
lattice, we have worked out identities for other lattices, which are concluded in Table
1
.

Table 1.
Identities of the form
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{N^{\prime}}+\theta_{0,0}^{N^{\prime}}=\theta_{\alpha,0}^{N^{\prime}}+\theta_{0,\beta}^{N^{\prime}}
found and proved by Theorem
7.10
, where
\alpha
’s (
\beta
’s resp.) means the number of
\alpha
’s (
\beta
’s resp.) and
\lvert\mathfrak{I}\rvert
equals the length of each vector
\Theta_{\alpha,\beta}

Lattice

N^{\prime}

’s

’s

Section
7
is devoted to this application. We first prove that (Proposition
7.6
) the functions
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}
are Jacobi forms, more exactly, in the space
J_{\mathfrak{n}/2,\underline{L}}(\Gamma_{1}(N),\chi_{\alpha,\beta})
, for any even integral lattice
\underline{L}=(L,B)
, where
\chi_{\alpha,\beta}
is certain linear character depending on
\alpha
and
\beta
. Then in Proposition
7.9
, we answer the question for which
\alpha
and
\beta
, functions
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}^{N^{\prime}}
are in the same space, that is, with the same character. Immediately after that, we state and prove the main theorem of this section, Theorem
7.10
. The remaining of this section is divided into five subsections. Each of the first three subsections deals with one lattice in Table
1
. In §
7.4
, we present a non-trivial example showing that, in Theorem
6.2
, we can not choose the set of
\mathbf{p}
’s with small cardinality. In §
7.5
, we show that the third (fifth) powers of nine (twenty-five) functions
\theta_{\alpha,\beta}
associated with the quadratic form
x^{2}+xy+4y^{2}
are linearly independent.

In Appendix
A
, we present the complete SageMath source code used to produce and check linear relations among series for the
D_{4}
lattice, along with detailed discussion on the usage and on how to fit the program for other lattices.

1.3.
Notations

The symbols
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
denote the ring of integers, rationals, reals and complex numbers respectively. The symbol
R_{>n}
(
R_{\geq n}
resp.) refers to the set of elements in
R
that are greater than (or equal to)
n
, The symbol
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
denotes the multiplicative group of nonzero complex numbers. The symbol
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q})
denotes the set of projective lines over
\mathbb{Q}
(
\mathbb{Q}
-subspaces of dimension one), whose elements can be represented by formal fractions
a/b
.

For a commutative ring
R
(with multiplicative identity), the general linear group
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(R)
is the group of
2\times 2
matrices over
R
with invertible determinant, and the special linear group
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(R)
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(R)
consisting of matrices of determinant
1
. If
R
is a subring of
\mathbb{R}
, then the symbol
\mathrm{GL}^{+}_{2}(R)
denote the subgroup consisting of matrices of positive determinant. For a subgroup
G
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
,
\widetilde{G}
denote its double cover (see the second paragraph of Section
2
), and
\overline{G}
denotes
G/\{\pm I\}
if
-I\in G
, or
G
itself if
-I\notin G
, where
I=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{0}\\
{0}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. We put
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\overline{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
.

Throughout this paper,
V
always denotes a linear space of dimension
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and
\mathcal{V}
its complexification
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}V
. The upper half plane
\mathfrak{H}
, is the subset of
\mathbb{C}
consisting of numbers with positive imaginary part. Besides
\mathcal{V}
, the symbol
\mathcal{W}
always denotes a complex linear space of dimension
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. These sets serve as domains and codomains of Jacobi forms considered here — a Jacobi form is implicitly assumed as a function from
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
to
\mathcal{W}
. See the first paragraph of Section
2
. By
C(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
,
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
, we mean the set of continuous maps, and that of holomorphic maps from
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
to
\mathcal{W}
. By
\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{W})
, we mean the group of non-singular linear operators on
\mathcal{W}
.

The symbols
H(\underline{V})
,
H(\underline{L},{n})
denote some kind of Heisenberg groups. Refer to the paragraph following the proof of Lemma
2.2
for details. The symbol
L
usually denotes a
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice in
V
, which is assumed to be integral at most time, and
\underline{L}
means
L
equipped with a bilinear form
B
(i.e.,
\underline{L}=(L,B)
). By
L^{\sharp}
, or more precisely,
\underline{L}^{\sharp}
, we mean the dual lattice of
L
(see the paragraph followed by Proposition
2.4
). For a positive integer
N
, the notation
\underline{L}_{N}
denotes the lattice with the same underlying
\mathbb{Z}
-module
L
, but a different bilinear form
N\cdot B
.

For simplicity, put
\mathfrak{e}(z)=\exp{2\uppi\mathrm{i}z}
with
z\in\mathbb{C}
, and
q^{n}=\mathfrak{e}\left(n\tau\right)
with
\tau\in\mathfrak{H},\,n\in\mathbb{Q}
. The notation
\Gamma(z)
refers to the Euler
\Gamma
-function. For two maps
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
,
f_{1}\circ f_{2}
means the map that sends
x
to
f_{1}(f_{2}(x))
, provided that the codomain of
f_{2}
coincides with the domain of
f_{1}
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the floor
[x]
equals the largest integer not exceeding
x
. For two groups
G
and
H
where
H
is a subgroup of
G
,
[G\colon H]
means the index of
H
in
G
. For a nonzero integer
d
,
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{d}{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}\left(d\right)
denotes the sign of
d
, and
\genfrac{(}{)}{}{}{0}{-1}=1
by convention.

Finally, for notations concerning formal Laurent series, refer to first two paragraphs of Section
3
.

2.
Jacobi forms of lattice index

We recall basic concepts and properties concerning
Jacobi forms of lattice index
. The readers may consult, for instance,
[
Ajo15
, §2.4]
,
[
Boy15
, §3.6]
,
[
GSZ19
, §9]
,
[
Moc19
, §2.2]
,
[
Wil19
, §2]
for more details. Throughout this paper, the symbol
V
always denotes a finite dimensional real vector space (whose dimension will be denoted by
\mathfrak{n}
), and
\mathcal{V}
its complexification, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}=V\oplus\mathrm{i}V
. Instead of
v+\mathrm{i}w
, we use the notation
[v,w]
to represent elements in
\mathcal{V}
, where
v,w\in V
.The symbol
\mathcal{W}
denotes another finite dimensional complex vector space of complex dimension
\mathfrak{m}
. The functions studied here are holomorphic ones from
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
to
\mathcal{W}
, where
\mathfrak{H}
denotes the upper half plane of the complex numbers. Let
B\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a symmetric
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear form and by abuse of language,
B
also denotes its
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear extension to
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}
. For any additive subgroup
X
of
\mathcal{V}
, we write
\underline{X}
to mean the group
X
equipped with the restriction of the form
B
to
X\times{}X
, so
\underline{\mathcal{V}}=(\mathcal{V},B)
. There is another “extension” of
B
on
V\times V
to
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}
, which is denoted by
A
and is defined by
A([v_{1},w_{1}],[v_{2},w_{2}])=B(v_{1},w_{2})-B(v_{2},w_{1})
. The form
A
on
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}
is alternating (anti-symmetric). We shall use
Q
to denote the quadratic form induced by
B
, that is,
Q(v)=\frac{1}{2}B(v,v)
with
v\in\mathcal{V}
.

To state the modular and abelian transformation laws, and to define operators such as
Hecke operators
and
double coset operators
, we need a group, whose elements represent transformations of functions studied in this paper. The group used here is the semidirect product of a general linear group and a
Heisenberg group
, which we now explain in detail. Let
\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
be the group of
2\times 2
real matrices with positive determinants,
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
be the subgroup of matrices of determinant
1
, and
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
be the subgroup of matrices with integeral entries in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. To deal with half-integer-weight modular forms, we shall use the double cover of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, which is denoted by
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}
, whose elements are of the form
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\left({c}^{\prime}\tau+{d}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)
, where
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
,
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, and
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a^{\prime}}&{b^{\prime}}\\
{c^{\prime}}&{d^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
=
\left(\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Note that by the function
z\mapsto z^{r}
, we mean
z\mapsto\exp{r\log z}
, where we choose the branch of
\log
such that
-\uppi<\Im\log z\leq\uppi
, and that
\varepsilon\left(c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
means a function on
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}
. For an element
\gamma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, we define
j(\gamma,\tau)=c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime}
, and
\gamma(\tau)=\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}=\frac{a^{\prime}\tau+b^{\prime}}{c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime}}
. The composition of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}
is given by

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a_{1}}&{b_{1}}\\
{c_{1}}&{d_{1}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon_{1}}\left({c_{1}}^{\prime}\tau+{d_{1}}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\cdot\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a_{2}}&{b_{2}}\\
{c_{2}}&{d_{2}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon_{2}}\left({c_{2}}^{\prime}\tau+{d_{2}}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\\
=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
c_{1}&d_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a_{2}&b_{2}\\
c_{2}&d_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\left(c_{1}^{\prime}\frac{a_{2}^{\prime}\tau+b_{2}^{\prime}}{c_{2}^{\prime}\tau+d_{2}^{\prime}}+d_{1}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(c_{2}^{\prime}\tau+d_{2}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

For any subgroup
G
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, the notation
\widetilde{G}
means the preimage of
G
under the natural projection from
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}
onto
\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, so we have
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
. For matrix
\gamma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, the symbol
\widetilde{\gamma}
means
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),(c\tau+d)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)
. Moreover, we sometimes write
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\right)
instead of
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\left({c}^{\prime}\tau+{d}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)
. For more details on such metaplectic groups, see
[
Bru02
, §1.1]
.

By the Heisenberg group
H(\underline{V})
, we mean the group of elements
([v,w],\xi)
with
[v,w]\in\mathcal{V}
and
\xi\in S^{1}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\lvert z\rvert=1\}
. The composition law is given by

([v_{1},w_{1}],\xi_{1})\cdot([v_{2},w_{2}],\xi_{2})\\
=([v_{1}+v_{2},w_{1}+w_{2}],\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\exp{\uppi\mathrm{i}A([v_{1},w_{1}],[v_{2},w_{2}])}).

The group
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}
acts from right on
H(\underline{V})
as follows:

\displaystyle H(\underline{V})\times\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\rightarrow H(\underline{V})

\displaystyle\left(\left([v,w],\xi\right),\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\left({c}^{\prime}\tau+{d}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left([v,w]\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a^{\prime}}&{b^{\prime}}\\
{c^{\prime}}&{d^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\xi\right),

where
[v,w]\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a^{\prime}}&{b^{\prime}}\\
{c^{\prime}}&{d^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
means
[a^{\prime}v+c^{\prime}w,b^{\prime}v+d^{\prime}w]
. Moreover, since

A([v_{1},w_{1}]\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a^{\prime}}&{b^{\prime}}\\
{c^{\prime}}&{d^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),[v_{2},w_{2}]\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a^{\prime}}&{b^{\prime}}\\
{c^{\prime}}&{d^{\prime}}\end{smallmatrix}\right))=A([v_{1},w_{1}],[v_{2},w_{2}]),

each element of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}
induces a group isomorphism of
H(\underline{V})
. Hence we can define the semi-direct product
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
, using the above action. The elements in
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
are written in the form
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\left({c}^{\prime}\tau+{d}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},[v,w],\xi\right)
. For
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}
, we always use the same notation to express
(\gamma,[0,0],1)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
. Similarly, the symbol
([v,w],\xi)\in H(\underline{V})
sometimes means corresponding
\left(\widetilde{I},[v,w],\xi\right)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
, where
I
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix.

The group
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
acts from left on the domain
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
as follows:

(2.1)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V}))\times(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}

\displaystyle\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\left({c}^{\prime}\tau+{d}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},[v,w],\xi\right),(\tau,z)\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\frac{a^{\prime}\tau+b^{\prime}}{c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime}},\frac{z+\tau v+w}{c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime}}\right).

We use the notation
\gamma Z
with
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
and
Z=(\tau,z)\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
to denote the resulting point when
\gamma
acts on
Z
.

Besides
j(\gamma,\tau)
, We need another
automorphic factor
, which is denoted by
J_{B}
, and is defined as follows:

\displaystyle J_{B}\colon\left(\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})\right)\times(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}

\displaystyle\left(\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\left({c}^{\prime}\tau+{d}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},[v,w],\xi\right),(\tau,z)\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathfrak{e}\left(-\frac{c^{\prime}}{c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime}}Q(z+\tau v+w)+\tau Q(v)+B(v,z)+\frac{1}{2}B(v,w)\right).

Lemma 2.1
.

The function
J_{B}
satisfies the cocycle condition. That is to say, for
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
and
Z\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
, one has

J_{B}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2},Z)=J_{B}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}Z)\cdot J_{B}(\gamma_{2},Z).

Proof.

A tedious but straightforward calculation only using definitions.
∎

Now we come to a position to define the transformations on functions form
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
to
\mathcal{W}
, using the group
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
. The symbols
C(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
,
C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
and
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
denote the (complex) spaces of continuous functions, smooth functions, and holomorphic functions respectively. Then
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
acts from right on
C(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
. To give this action, let
\gamma=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\left({c}^{\prime}\tau+{d}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},[v,w],\xi\right)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
,
Z=(\tau,z)\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
and
f\in C(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
. Then

(f|_{k,B}\gamma)(Z)=\left(\varepsilon\sqrt{c^{\prime}\tau+d^{\prime}}\right)^{-2k}J_{B}(\gamma,Z)f(\gamma Z).

In the above formula,
k
is a number called the
weight
, and
|_{k,B}
is called the
slash operator
of weight
k
and form
B
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
k\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
. Then the slash operater is a right group action; that is, for
f\in C(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
and
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
, we have
\left(f|_{k,B}\gamma_{1}\right)|_{k,B}\gamma_{2}=f|_{k,B}(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})
, and the identity element of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
gives the identity map on
C(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
.

Note that when we restrict the set of functions to
C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
, or to
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
, this action still remains well-defined.

Proof.

This follows from that
j(\gamma,\tau)
and
J_{B}(\gamma,Z)
satisfy cocycle condition (The case of
j(\gamma,\tau)
is classical, and the case of
J_{B}(\gamma,Z)
is in Lemma
2.1
.), and that (
2.1
) is a left group action.
∎

By a
\mathbb{Z}
-
lattice

L
in
\underline{V}
, we mean a free
\mathbb{Z}
- module in
\underline{V}
, with a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis which is also a
\mathbb{R}
-basis of
V
. As mentioned above, set
\underline{L}=(L,B|_{L\times L})
. We say that
L
is
integral
if
B(L,L)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, and
L
is
even
if
Q(L)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
. If
L
is integral but not even, then we say
L
is
odd
. Set
H(\underline{L})=\{([v,w],\xi)\in H(\underline{V})\colon v,w\in L\}
. Then
H(\underline{L})
is a subgroup of
H(\underline{V})
, and for any subgroup
G
of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, the set
\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L})
is a subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
. If
B(L,L)\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
, we can make the Heisenberg group a cover of
L\times L
of finite index. To do this, define
H(\underline{L},{n})=\{([v,w],\xi)\in H(\underline{L})\colon\xi^{2n}=1\}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. This is a subgroup when
B(L,L)\subseteq\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}
. Particularly, if
L
is integral, then
H(\underline{L},{1})
is a subgroup, which is used to state the elliptic transformation laws for Jacobi forms. Though there is a subgroup of
H(\underline{L})
that is isomorphic to
L\times L
when
L
is even, we shall not use this subgroup for the sake of dealing with even and odd lattices simultaneously. Note that
H(\underline{L},{1})
is abelian.

Convention 1
.

From now on, we always assume that
L
is an integral lattice, and
G
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. So
\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
is a subgroup of
\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(\underline{V})
. Moreover,
k
always denotes a half integer (or an integer), and the weight of Jacobi forms considered is assumed to be
k
, unless explicitly specified.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\rho\colon\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{W})
be a group representation, and
\phi\in C(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
. We say that
\phi
transforms like a Jacobi form of weight
k
and index
\underline{L}
under the group
G
with the representation
\rho
, if for any
\gamma\in\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
, we have
\phi|_{k,B}\gamma=\rho(\gamma)\circ\phi
. Denote the space of such functions by
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{cont.}(G,\rho)
, and the subspace of holomorphic ones by
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{\mathcal{O}}(G,\rho)
.

Note that the slash operator and applying a linear operator on the left commute, i.e.,
(L\circ\phi)|_{k,B}\gamma=L\circ(\phi|_{k,B}\gamma)
for any linear operator
L
on
\mathcal{W}
. As a consequence, to show that a map
\phi
transforms like a Jacobi form, it suffices to verify the tansformation laws corresponding to a set of generators of
\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
.

To make spaces of such (holomorphic) functions finite-dimensional, hence arithmetically interesting, we shall pose growth conditions at cusps of the quotient
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
modulo
\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
, which will be given by Fourier series. So we recall some fundamental facts about Fourier series on the
\mathfrak{n}
-torus, stated in a form suits us. Assume that
B
(on
V\times V
) is nondegenerate, that is, the map
v\mapsto(w\mapsto B(v,w))
is a linear isomorphism from
V
onto the dual space of
V
. Then the function
V\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad x\mapsto\mathfrak{e}\left(B(v,x)\right)
is
L
-periodic, where
v
is a vector in the dual lattice
L^{\sharp}
of
L
in
\underline{V}
. (The
dual lattice

L^{\sharp}
is defined to be the subset of
v\in V
such that
B(v,L)\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, which turns out to be a lattice of the same rank if
B
is nondegenerate. To indicate the bilinear form, we also wrtie
\underline{L}^{\sharp}
.) On the other hand, there is an inner product on
V
induced by
L
, given by
(f,g)=\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\left(\mathfrak{F}\right)^{-1}\int_{\mathfrak{F}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x
, where
f
and
g
are complex-valued continuous
L
-periodic functions, and
\mathfrak{F}
is any closed fundamental domain being the preimage of
[0,1]^{\mathfrak{n}}
under a linear isomorphism from
V
onto
\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{n}}
which is simultaneously an isomorphism of
L
and
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
(recall that
\mathfrak{n}=\dim V
). This inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
, is independent of the choice of
\mathfrak{F}
and the Lebesgue measure posed on
V
. A fundamental theorem in Fourier analysis asserts that any complex-valued continuous
L
-periodic function
f
on
V
can be decomposed as
f(x)=\sum_{t\in L^{\sharp}}a_{t}\mathfrak{e}\left(B(t,x)\right)
with
a_{t}\in\mathbb{C}
, where the equality means that the right-hand side converges unconditionally with repect to the inner product to the left-hand side. Put another way, the functions
\{x\mapsto\mathfrak{e}\left(B(t,x)\right)\colon t\in L^{\sharp}\}
forms a maximal orthonormal basis of the space of complex-valued continuous
L
-periodic functions equipped with the inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
. Since we deal mainly with holomorphic functions, the following proposition should be useful.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
be a complex vector space of dimension
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{d})
be a basis. Set
V^{\prime}=\oplus_{1\leq j\leq d}\mathbb{R}e_{j}
. Let
D
be a domain (connected open set) in
V^{\prime}
, and
B^{\prime}\colon V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form, which extends
\mathbb{C}
-linearly to
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
. Let
L^{\prime}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice in
(V^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, and
f\colon V^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{i}D\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a holomorphic function. Suppose that
f(z+v)=f(z)
for any
z\in V^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{i}D
and
v\in L^{\prime}
. Then there exists a unique sequence
c_{t}
for
t\in L^{\prime\sharp}
, such that

f(z)=\sum_{t\in L^{\prime\sharp}}c_{t}\mathfrak{e}\left(B^{\prime}(t,z)\right).

The series converges normally on
V^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{i}D
. The coefficients are given by

c_{t}=\frac{1}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\left(\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}\right)}\int_{\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}}f(x+\mathrm{i}y)\mathfrak{e}\left(-B(t,x+\mathrm{i}y)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
y\in D
, the integral is with respect to the Lebesgue measure transformed from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
via the coordinate map from
V^{\prime}
onto
\mathbb{R}^{d}
induced by the basis
(e_{j})_{1\leq j\leq d}
, and
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}
is the inverse image of
[0,1]^{d}
under this coordinate map.

Proof.

For the case of
B^{\prime}
being positive definite, see
[
Fre11
, Appendix to §6.8]
. The general case follows from this special case.
∎

By saying that a function series
\sum_{t}f_{t}
defined on
V^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{i}D
converges normally, we mean that for any compact subset
K
of
V^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{i}D
, we have
\sum_{t}\sup_{x\in K}\lvert f_{t}(x)\rvert<\infty
. This implies absolute convergence and compactly uniform convergence. But normal convergence is stronger.
Note that this proposition generalizes immediately to functions taking values in finite-dimensional complex vector spaces. In this situation, in the definition of normal convergence, we shall substitute the absolute value by the norm. Since any norms on a finite-dimensional real or complex vector space are equivalent, we need not refer to the norm when talking about normal convergence. For us, the proposition applies to the case
V^{\prime}=\mathbb{R}\times V
,
D=\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times V
,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}\times\mathcal{V}
, so
V^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{i}D
is
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Use the notations in Definition
2.3
. Suppose that the index of
G
in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is finite,
B
is positive definite, and
\ker\rho
is of finite index in
\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
. Put
N_{1}=[\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}:\widetilde{G}]
and
N_{2}=[\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1}):\ker\rho]
. Then for any
\phi\in J_{k,\underline{L}}^{\mathcal{O}}(G,\rho)
and
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
, there exist a
\mathcal{W}
-valued sequence
c(n,t)
with
n\in\frac{1}{N_{1}N_{2}}\mathbb{Z}
and
t\in\frac{1}{N_{1}N_{2}}L^{\sharp}
, such that

(2.2)

\phi|_{k,B}\gamma(\tau,z)=\sum_{n,\,t}c(n,t)q^{n}\mathfrak{e}\left(B(t,z)\right).

This series converges normally. As a consequence, it converges absolutely and locally uniformly at any
(\tau,z)\in\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

Put
f=\phi|_{k,B}\gamma
. We shall show that

f(\tau+N_{1}N_{2}u,z+N_{1}N_{2}v)=f(\tau,z)

for
u\in\mathbb{Z}
and
v\in L
. Then (
2.2
) follows from this and Proposition
2.4
. Note that
T=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{1}\\
{0}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
as usual in the theory of elliptic modular forms. We have

\displaystyle f(\tau+N_{1}N_{2}u,z+N_{1}N_{2}v)

\displaystyle=\phi|_{k,B}\gamma|_{k,B}\widetilde{T}^{N_{1}N_{2}u}([0,v],1)^{N_{1}N_{2}}(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=\phi|_{k,B}(\gamma\widetilde{T}^{u}\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}N_{2}}|_{k,B}(\gamma([0,v],1)\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}N_{2}}|_{k,B}\gamma(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=\rho((\gamma\widetilde{T}^{u}\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}N_{2}})\circ\rho((\gamma([0,v],1)\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}N_{2}})\circ f(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=f(\tau,z).

In the last step of above dedection, we have use the fact
(\gamma\widetilde{T}^{u}\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}}\in\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
and
(\gamma([0,v],1)\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}}\in\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
, which is a consequence of the definition of
N_{1}
, and the fact
\rho((\gamma\widetilde{T}^{u}\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}N_{2}})=\rho((\gamma([0,v],1)\gamma^{-1})^{N_{1}N_{2}})
are both the identity map on
\mathcal{W}
, which is a consequence of the definition of
N_{2}
.
∎

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\rho\colon\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{W})
be a group representation and
\phi\in J_{k,\underline{L}}^{\mathcal{O}}(G,\rho)
. Suppose that
[\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}):G]<\infty
,
[\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1}):\ker\rho]<\infty
and
B
is positive definite. We say
\phi
is a
weakly holomorphic Jacobi form
, if for any
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
, there exists an
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that in the series (
2.2
),
c(n,t)=0
unless
n\geq N_{0}
. We say that
\phi
is a
weak Jacobi form
, if it is weakly holomorphic with
N_{0}=0
for any
\gamma
. And we say that
\phi
is a
Jacobi form
(
Jacobi cusp form
, resp.), if
c(n,t)=0
unless
n-Q(t)\geq 0
(
n-Q(t)>0
resp.) in the series (
2.2
) for any
\gamma
. The spaces of weakly holomorphic forms, weak forms, Jacobi forms, Jacobi cusp forms are denoted by
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{!}(G,\rho)
,
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{weak}(G,\rho)
,
J_{k,\underline{L}}(G,\rho)
and
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{cusp}(G,\rho)
respectively.

In practice, one need only check the expansion (
2.2
) for finitely many
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
:

Proposition 2.7
.

Use the notations and assumptions in Definition
2.6
. Let
\{s_{i}\colon 1\leq i\leq w\}
be a system of representatives of the quotient
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q})
modulo
G
. Suppose that
\gamma_{i}\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\gamma_{i}(\infty)=s_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq w
. Suppose
\phi\in J_{k,\underline{L}}^{\mathcal{O}}(G,\rho)
. Then
\phi\in J_{k,\underline{L}}(G,\rho)
if and only if
\phi|_{k,B}\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}
has an expansion of the form (
2.2
) with
n-Q(t)\geq 0
for each
i
. There are similar conclusions for
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{!}(G,\rho)
,
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{weak}(G,\rho)
and
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{cusp}(G,\rho)
.

Still use the notations and assumptions in Definition
2.6
. We give some properties on Fourier coefficients, some of which are used in the proof of Proposition
2.7
. We use
c^{\gamma}(n,t)
, or more precisely
c^{\gamma}_{\phi}(n,t)
to denote the coefficients
c(n,t)
in (
2.2
), to indicate that it depends on
\gamma
and
\phi
. It is useful to extend the domain of the map
(n,t)\mapsto c^{\gamma}(n,t)
to
\mathbb{R}\times V
, such that
c^{\gamma}(n,t)=0
unless
n\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}
and
t\in\frac{1}{N}L^{\sharp}
(
N=N_{1}N_{2}
). Let
v\in L
and
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
; applying the operator
|_{k,B}([v,0],1)
to
\phi|_{k,B}\gamma
on the right, computing the Fourier coefficients in two ways and using the uniquessness of Fourier coefficients yield that

(2.3)

\rho([v,0]\gamma^{-1},1)\circ c^{\gamma}(n+Q(v)+B(t,v),t+v)=c^{\gamma}(n,t)

for any
(n,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times V
. A similar formula holds for
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
. As a consequence, for
\phi\in J_{k,\underline{L}}^{\mathcal{O}}(G,\rho)
and
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})
, if
c^{\gamma}_{\phi}(n,t)\neq 0
, then
c^{\gamma}_{\phi}(n+Q(v)+B(t,v),t+v)\neq 0
for any
v\in L
. Moreover, if
\phi\in J_{k,\underline{L}}^{!}(G,\rho)
, and
c^{\gamma}_{\phi}(n,t)\neq 0
implies that
n\geq N_{0}
with
N_{0}\in\mathbb{Q}
, then
c^{\gamma}_{\phi}(n,t)\neq 0
would imply that
n+Q(v)+B(t,v)\geq N_{0}
. Hence for any fixed
n\geq N_{0}
, the Fourier coefficient
c^{\gamma}_{\phi}(n,t)
can be nonzero only if
Q(t)\leq n-N_{0}+\max_{x\in\mathfrak{F}}\{Q(x)\}
, where
\mathfrak{F}
is the same as in the paragraph previous to Proposition
2.4
. Now we can give the proof of Proposition
2.7
.

Proof.

The “only if” part is obvious, and the proofs for
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{!}(G,\rho)
,
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{weak}(G,\rho)
and
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{cusp}(G,\rho)
are similar to that for
J_{k,\underline{L}}(G,\rho)
. Hence we only prove the “if” part for
J_{k,\underline{L}}(G,\rho)
. Suppose that
\phi|_{k,B}\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}
has an expansion of the form (
2.2
) with
n-Q(t)\geq 0
for each
i
, where the Fourier coefficient
c(n,t)
is denoted by
c_{i}(n,t)
here, to indicate its dependence on
\gamma_{i}
. Let

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],\xi\right)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})

be arbitrary, and put
\gamma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. We shall prove that
\phi|_{k,B}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],\xi\right)
has an expansion of the form (
2.2
) with the property
c(n,t)\neq 0\implies n-Q(t)\geq 0
. Suppose
\gamma(\infty)
is equivalent to
s_{i}
modulo
G
, so there is some
\gamma^{\prime}\in G
such that
\gamma(\infty)=\gamma^{\prime}(s_{i})
. Thus,
\gamma_{i}^{-1}\gamma^{\prime-1}\gamma(\infty)=\infty
, and in consequence, there is some
e\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\delta=\pm 1
such that
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma_{i}(\delta T^{e})
, where
T=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{1}\\
{0}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Hence, there is some
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\pm 1
such that

\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],\xi\right)=(\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}(\delta T^{e},1,[v,w],\xi).

It follows that

\displaystyle\phi|_{k,B}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],\xi\right)(\tau,z)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\phi|_{k,B}(\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})|_{k,B}\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}|_{k,B}(\delta T^{e},1,[v,w],\xi)(\tau,z)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\delta^{-k}\xi\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{1}{2}B(v,w)\right)\sum_{n,\,t}\rho(\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})(c_{i}(n,t))\mathfrak{e}\left(e\cdot n+B(\delta t,w)\right)

\displaystyle\times q^{n+Q(v)+B(\delta t,v)}\mathfrak{e}\left(B(\delta t+v,z)\right).

Using the change of variables corresponding to the following bijection

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times V

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times V

\displaystyle(n,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(n+Q(v)+B(\delta t,v),\delta t+v),

and applying (
2.3
), we obtain

c(n,t)=\delta^{-k}\xi\mathfrak{e}\left(-\frac{1}{2}B(v,w)+e\cdot(n+Q(v)-B(t,v))+B(t,w)\right)\\
\times\rho(\gamma^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime})\circ\rho([\delta v,0]\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}^{-1},1)\circ c_{i}(n,\delta t).

Now if
n-Q(t)=n-Q(\delta t)<0
, then
c_{i}(n,\delta t)=0
, and hence
c(n,t)=0
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Convention 2
.

This paper is aimed at investigating embeddings of
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{\mathcal{O}}(G,\rho)
,
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{!}(G,\rho)
,
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{weak}(G,\rho)
,
J_{k,\underline{L}}(G,\rho)
and
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{cusp}(G,\rho)
into certain direct product of spaces of modular forms. Thus, we will at most time assume that
\rho\colon\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{W})
is a group representation,
[\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}):G]<\infty
,
[\widetilde{G}\ltimes H(\underline{L},{1}):\ker\rho]<\infty
and
B
is positive definite. Moreover, we require that
\rho([0,0],-1)
is the map sending
w\in\mathcal{W}
to
-w
for the obvious reason.

We conclude this section by recalling another basic property which would be used later, concerned with multiplication of two scalar-valued Jacobi forms of lattice index.

Suppose
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are two positive definite symmetric
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear forms on
V
. (As usual,
Q_{i}(x)=\frac{1}{2}B_{i}(x,x)
for
i=1,2
.) Then their sum
B_{1}+B_{2}
, defined pointwisely, is also positive definite symmetric. Let
L_{i}^{\sharp}
denote the dual lattice of
L
in the inner product space
(V,B_{i})
(
i=1,2
), and
L_{3}^{\sharp}
the dual of
L
in
(V,B_{1}+B_{2})
, where
L
is any
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice in
V
, not necessary integral.

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. For any
t_{1}\in\frac{1}{N}L_{1}^{\sharp}
and
t_{2}\in\frac{1}{N}L_{2}^{\sharp}
, there exists a unique
t\in V
such that

\mathfrak{e}\left((B_{1}+B_{2})(t,x)\right)=\mathfrak{e}\left(B_{1}(t_{1},x)\right)\mathfrak{e}\left(B_{2}(t_{2},x)\right)

for any
x\in V
. Moreover, this
t
belongs to
\frac{1}{N}L_{3}^{\sharp}
, and can be obtained by the following way. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a
\mathbb{R}
-basis of
V
, and
(t_{1}^{1},\dots,t_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}})
,
(t_{2}^{1},\dots,t_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}})
be coordinate row vectors of
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
with respect to
\mathfrak{B}
, respectively. Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be the Gram matrices of
B_{1},B_{2}
with respect to the basis
\mathfrak{B}
, respectively. Then the coordinate row vector
(t^{1},\dots,t^{\mathfrak{n}})
of
t
is uniquely determined by the following matrix equation

(t^{1},\dots,t^{\mathfrak{n}})=((t_{1}^{1},\dots,t_{1}^{\mathfrak{n}})G_{1}+(t_{2}^{1},\dots,t_{2}^{\mathfrak{n}})G_{2})(G_{1}+G_{2})^{-1}.

Proof.

A direct verification.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Use notations and assumptions in the last lemma. Put
G^{\prime}=G_{1}(G_{1}+G_{2})^{-1}G_{2}
, then
G^{\prime}
is a positive definite symmetric matrix. Moreover, let
B^{\prime}
denote the bilinear form on
V\times V
whose Gram matrix with respect to
\mathfrak{B}
is
G^{\prime}
, and put
Q^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{2}B^{\prime}(x,x)
. Then we have

Q_{1}(t_{1})+Q_{2}(t_{2})-(Q_{1}+Q_{2})(t)=Q^{\prime}(t_{1}-t_{2}).

As a consequence,
(Q_{1}+Q_{2})(t)\leq Q_{1}(t_{1})+Q_{2}(t_{2})
.

Proof.

A direct verification.
∎

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
k_{1},k_{2}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
and
B_{1},B_{2}
be two positive definite symmetric
\mathbb{R}
-bilinear forms on
V
. Let
L
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-lattice in
V
that is integral both in
(V,B_{1})
and
(V,B_{2})
. Let
G
be a finite index subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\chi_{i}
be a linear character on
\widetilde{G}\ltimes H((L,B_{i}),1)
whose kernel is of finite index, for
i=1,2
. It is required that
\chi_{i}([0,0],\xi)=\xi
. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\colon\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be two functions. Then we have following conclusions.

(1)

The map

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}\ltimes H((L,B_{1}+B_{2}),1)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}

\displaystyle\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],\xi\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\xi\chi_{1}\cdot\chi_{2}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],1\right)

is a linear character, which is denoted by
\chi_{1}\ast\chi_{2}
. The notation
\chi_{1}\cdot\chi_{2}
denotes the pointwise multiplication.

(2)

If
f_{i}\in J_{k_{i},(L,B_{i})}(G,\chi_{i})
for
i=1,2
, then

f_{1}\cdot f_{2}\in J_{k_{1}+k_{2},(L,B_{1}+B_{2})}(G,\chi_{1}\ast\chi_{2}).

Moreover, if one of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
is a Jacobi cusp form, then so is
f_{1}\cdot f_{2}
.

Proof.

The conclusion on
\chi_{1}\ast\chi_{2}
can be proved by the definition of linear characters. Just pay attention to the fact that underlying group compositions of
H((L,B_{1}),1)
,
H((L,B_{2}),1)
and
H((L,B_{1}+B_{2}),1)
are different. Now suppose
f_{i}\in J_{k_{i},(L,B_{i})}(G,\chi_{i})
for
i=1,2
. We have

f_{1}\cdot f_{2}|_{k_{1}+k_{2},B_{1}+B_{2}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],\xi\right)\\
=\xi\cdot f_{1}|_{k_{1},B_{1}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],1\right)f_{2}|_{k_{2},B_{2}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],1\right)

for any
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon},[v,w],\xi\right)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{GL}_{2}^{+}(\mathbb{R})}\ltimes H(V,B_{1}+B_{2})
, from which the transformation laws (see Definition
2.3
) follow. The requirement on Fourier expansions (see Definition
2.6
) follows from Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

There are also parallel conclusions for weakly holomorphic Jacobi forms, and for weak Jacobi forms.

3.
Formal Laurent series which transform like modular forms

For the sake of freely defining differential operators used to construct embeddings of spaces of Jacobi forms into products of spaces of modular forms, we shift the focus from functions in
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
to formal series whose coefficients are modular forms in
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})
. This section is devoted to developing such a theory. Let
T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{\mathfrak{n}}
be formal variables. Let
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{\mathfrak{n}})
be in
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
and set
T^{\mathbf{j}}=T_{1}^{j_{1}}T_{2}^{j_{2}}\cdots T_{\mathfrak{n}}^{j_{\mathfrak{n}}}
. Use the symbol
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
to denote the set of all formal series whose terms are of the form
h_{\mathbf{j}}(\tau)T^{\mathbf{j}}
with
h_{\mathbf{j}}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})
for each
\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
. This set becomes a
\mathbb{C}
-space, also a
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathbb{C})
-module under the ordinary addition and scalar multiplication. The symbol
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
denotes the subspace of those series
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
with the property there exists a
\mathbf{j}_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
such that
h_{\mathbf{j}}\neq 0
implies
2
2

2

By
\mathbf{j}\geq\mathbf{j}_{0}
, we mean each component of
\mathbf{j}
is not less than that of
\mathbf{j}_{0}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{j}>\mathbf{j}_{0}
means
\mathbf{j}\geq\mathbf{j}_{0}
but
\mathbf{j}\neq\mathbf{j}_{0}
.

\mathbf{j}\geq\mathbf{j}_{0}
. Equipped with the ordinary multiplication, the subspace
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra (associative and unitary). We also need the subspace
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\geq{\mathbf{j}_{0}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
with
\mathbf{j}_{0}
being a fixed vector.

Next we shall define slash operators on formal series. For this purpose, it is necessary to introduce some nonstandard notations. The symbol
{R}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}
denotes the set of all
\mathfrak{n}\times\mathfrak{n}
matrices with entries in
R
. For
G
,
\Lambda
in
{R}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}
,
s(G)
denotes the sum of all entries of
G
, and
G^{\Lambda}
denotes the product of all
g^{\lambda}
where
(g,\lambda)
ranges over corresponding entries in
G
and
\Lambda
. By
\Lambda_{\ast,j}
and
\Lambda_{i,\ast}
, we mean the
j
-th column and
i
-th row of
\Lambda
respectively. Put
\pi_{i}(\Lambda)=s(\Lambda_{i,\ast})+s(\Lambda_{\ast,i})
, and
\pi(\Lambda)=(\pi_{1}(\Lambda),\dots,\pi_{\mathfrak{n}}(\Lambda))
. We define

\Lambda!=\prod_{\lambda}\lambda!,

with
\lambda
ranging over all entries of
\Lambda
.For
\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
,
s(\mathbf{j})
denotes the sum of all components of
\mathbf{j}
, as in the matrix case. Under such notations, one has, for instance,

(3.4)

\frac{\left(\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq\mathfrak{n}}g_{ij}T_{i}T_{j}\right)^{s}}{s!}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}}\\
{s(\Lambda)=s}\end{subarray}}\frac{G^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}T^{\pi(\Lambda)},

where
g_{i,j}
is the
(i,j)
-entry of
G\in{\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}
, and
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{\mathfrak{n}})
be an ordered
\mathbb{R}
-basis of V, and let
G_{\mathfrak{B}}
be the Gram matrix of
B
with respect to
\mathfrak{B}
(i.e. the
(i,j)
-th entry is
B(e_{i},e_{j})
.). Let
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}
be a formal series in
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
, and
\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\right)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
. We define
\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\right)|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{g}_{\mathbf{p}}T^{\mathbf{p}}
, where

(3.5)

g_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau)=\varepsilon^{-2k}\sum_{\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}}\frac{(-\uppi\mathrm{i}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}(c\tau+d)^{-(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda))}h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right).

We warn the readers that
0^{0}=1
as usual in the theory of power series. Note that the sum used to define
g_{\mathbf{p}}
is actually a finite sum, since for
\Lambda
with
s(\Lambda)
sufficiently large, we have
h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}=0
. One can also verify that, the formal series
\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{g}_{\mathbf{p}}T^{\mathbf{p}}
is again in
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
, and if, in addition,
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in\sum_{\mathbf{j}\geq{\mathbf{j}_{0}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
, then
\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{g}_{\mathbf{p}}T^{\mathbf{p}}\in\sum_{\mathbf{j}\geq{\mathbf{j}_{0}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
too. We shall prove that this is a group action, for which the following technical lemma is useful.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}
, and
z_{1},\,z_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
. We have

\frac{1}{\Lambda!}(z_{1}+z_{2})^{s(\Lambda)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\Lambda_{1},\,\Lambda_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}}\\
{\lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}!\Lambda_{2}!}z_{1}^{s(\Lambda_{1})}z_{2}^{s(\Lambda_{2})}.

Proof.

By the binomial theorem, it suffices to prove that, for any non-negative integer
u,v
satisfying
u+v=s(\lambda)
, the following formula holds:

(3.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda}\\
{s(\Lambda_{1})=u,\,s(\Lambda_{2})=v}\end{subarray}}\frac{u!}{\Lambda_{1}!}\frac{v!}{\Lambda_{2}!}=\frac{(u+v)!}{\Lambda!}.

Let
X_{i,j}
be
\mathfrak{n}^{2}
indeterminants (
1\leq i,\,j\leq\mathfrak{n}
) and let
X
be the matrix whose
(i,j)
-th entry is
X_{i,j}
. It is trivial that

(3.7)

\left(\sum_{1\leq i,\,j\leq\mathfrak{n}}X_{i,j}\right)^{u+v}=\left(\sum_{1\leq i,\,j\leq\mathfrak{n}}X_{i,j}\right)^{u}\left(\sum_{1\leq i,\,j\leq\mathfrak{n}}X_{i,j}\right)^{v}.

Now evaluating the coefficients of
X^{\Lambda}
in both sides of the above formula leads to (
3.6
).
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

The slash operator given in Definition
3.1
is a right group action of
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
on
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
.

Proof.

The fact that identity element in
\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
gives the identity map on
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
is obvious. So we should prove that, for
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
, and
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
, the following formula holds:

(3.8)

\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\right)|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma_{1}|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma_{2}=\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\right)|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}.

We put
\gamma_{i}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a_{i}}&{b_{i}}\\
{c_{i}}&{d_{i}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon_{i}}\right)
for
i=1,\,2
and
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a_{3}}&{b_{3}}\\
{c_{3}}&{d_{3}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon_{3}}\right)
. Then for
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
, the coefficient of
T^{\mathbf{p}}
in the left-hand side of (
3.8
) is

(3.9)

\varepsilon_{1}^{-2k}\varepsilon_{2}^{-2k}\sum_{\Lambda_{1},\,\Lambda_{2}}\frac{(-\uppi\mathrm{i})^{s(\Lambda_{1})+s(\Lambda_{2})}c_{1}^{s(\Lambda_{1})}c_{2}^{s(\Lambda_{2})}}{\Lambda_{1}!\Lambda_{2}!}G_{\mathfrak{B}}^{\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}}\\
\cdot(c_{2}\tau+d_{2})^{-(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda_{2}))}\left(c_{1}\frac{a_{2}\tau+b_{2}}{c_{2}\tau+d_{2}}+d_{1}\right)^{-(k+s(\mathbf{p})-2s(\Lambda_{2})-s(\Lambda_{1}))}\\
\cdot h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda_{1})-\pi(\Lambda_{2})}\left(\frac{a_{3}\tau+b_{3}}{c_{3}\tau+d_{3}}\right).

For a fixed
\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}
, the subsum satisfying
\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda
in (
3.9
) is

(3.10)

\varepsilon_{3}^{-2k}\sum_{\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda}\frac{(-\uppi\mathrm{i})^{s(\Lambda)}}{\Lambda_{1}!\lambda_{2}!}G_{\mathfrak{B}}^{\Lambda}(c_{3}\tau+d_{3})^{-(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda))}\\
\cdot\left(\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}\tau+d_{2}}\right)^{s(\Lambda_{1})}\left(c_{2}(c_{1}\gamma_{2}\tau+d_{1})\right)^{s(\Lambda_{2})}h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}\left(\frac{a_{3}\tau+b_{3}}{c_{3}\tau+d_{3}}\right).

Using Lemma
3.2
, we have

\sum_{\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda}\frac{1}{\Lambda_{1}!\Lambda_{2}!}\left(\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}\tau+d_{2}}\right)^{s(\Lambda_{1})}\left(c_{2}(c_{1}\gamma_{2}\tau+d_{1})\right)^{s(\Lambda_{2})}=\frac{1}{\Lambda!}c_{3}^{s(\Lambda)}.

Inserting this into (
3.10
), we find that (
3.9
) equals the coefficient of
T^{\mathbf{p}}
in the right-hand side of (
3.8
), form which (
3.8
) follows.
∎

Definition
3.1
has an equivalent form, namely,

(3.11)

\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\right)|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\right)=\varepsilon^{-2k}(c\tau+d)^{-k}\\
\cdot\mathfrak{e}\left(-\frac{c}{c\tau+d}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m,n}a_{m,n}T_{m}T_{n}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}h_{\mathbf{j}}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)\left(\frac{T_{1}}{c\tau+d}\right)^{j_{1}}\dots\left(\frac{T_{\mathfrak{n}}}{c\tau+d}\right)^{j_{\mathfrak{n}}},

where
a_{m,n}
is the
(m,n)
-th entry of
G_{\mathfrak{B}}
. Maybe this form is more natural, since it coincides with the slash operators on Jacobi forms. See the paragraph that precedes Lemma
2.2
. In fact, it is from (
3.11
) and the power series expansion of the exponential function that we work out (
3.5
). We can also give an inverse formula expressing
h_{\mathbf{j}}
by
g_{\mathbf{p}}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}
and
\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{g}_{\mathbf{p}}T^{\mathbf{p}}
be two formal series in
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
. Then (
3.5
) holds for any
\mathbf{p}
if and only if the following relation holds for any
\mathbf{j}
:

(3.12)

h_{\mathbf{j}}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=\varepsilon^{2k}\sum_{\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}}\frac{(\uppi\mathrm{i}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}(c\tau+d)^{k+s(\mathbf{j})-s(\Lambda)}g_{\mathbf{j}-\pi(\Lambda)}(\tau).

Proof.

(
3.5
)
\implies
(
3.12
). Note that (
3.5
) and (
3.11
) are equivalent. Hence we have

\varepsilon^{-2k}(c\tau+d)^{-(k+s(\mathbf{j}))}\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}h_{\mathbf{j}}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)T^{\mathbf{j}}\\
=\mathfrak{e}\left(\frac{c}{c\tau+d}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m,n}a_{m,n}T_{m}T_{n}\right)\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}g_{\mathbf{p}}(\tau)T^{\mathbf{p}}.

Now expand the first factor in the right-hand side of the above identity as a power series of
T_{1},\dots,T_{\mathfrak{n}}
and compare the coefficients of
T^{\mathbf{j}}
in both sides; the desired relation then follows.

(
3.12
)
\implies
(
3.5
). Put
\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\right)|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\right)=\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{g^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{p}}T^{\mathbf{p}}
Then by the first part of this proposition (which we have proved),
h_{\mathbf{j}}
and
g^{\prime}_{\mathbf{p}}
also satisfy relation (
3.12
) with
g
replaced by
g^{\prime}
. By induction on
s(\mathbf{p})
one finds that
g_{\mathbf{p}}=g^{\prime}_{\mathbf{p}}
, for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
, from which
(
3.5
)
follows.
∎

We need a formula dealing with taking successive derivatives of (
3.12
), which is important in the proof of Proposition
4.15
, a main result of the next section.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}
be a formal series in
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
. Suppose
\sigma\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{W})
,
\gamma=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\varepsilon\right)\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
,
k\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
and
G_{\mathfrak{B}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}
. If the coefficient
h_{\mathbf{p}}
satisfy

h_{\mathbf{p}}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=\varepsilon^{2k}\sum_{\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}}\frac{(\uppi\mathrm{i}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}(c\tau+d)^{k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda)}\sigma\circ h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}(\tau),

then for any
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(3.13)

\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{d}^{u}}{h_{\mathbf{p}}}}{\mathop{\mathrm{d}}\tau^{u}}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=\varepsilon^{2k}\sum_{\Lambda\in{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}^{{\mathfrak{n}}\times{\mathfrak{n}}}}\frac{(\uppi\mathrm{i}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}(c\tau+d)^{k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda)}\\
\times\sum_{0\leq l\leq u}\binom{u}{l}\frac{\Gamma(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda)+u)}{\Gamma(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda)+l)}c^{u-l}(c\tau+d)^{u+l}\mathop{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{l}}}\sigma\circ h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}(\tau).

Proof.

Induction on
u
.
∎

We now describe connections between slash operators on Jacobi forms introduced in Section
2
and that on formal series introduced in Definition
3.1
. As above, let
\mathfrak{B}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{\mathfrak{n}})
be an ordered
\mathbb{R}
-basis of
V
. We define a
\mathbb{C}
-linear embedding, denoted by
\mathop{emb}_{\mathfrak{B}}
, of
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
to
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\geq{\mathbf{0}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
as follows. For
f\in\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
, we expand the function
f(\tau,z_{1}e_{1}+\dots z_{\mathfrak{n}}e_{\mathfrak{n}})
as a power series of
(z_{1},\dots,z_{\mathfrak{n}})\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathfrak{n}}
around zero. Then
\mathop{emb}_{\mathfrak{B}}(f)
is defined as the resulting power series with
z_{i}
’s replaced by
T_{i}
’s.

Proposition 3.6
.

For any
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
, the following diagram (of the category of complex vector spaces) commutes:

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|_{k,B}\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{emb}_{\mathfrak{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H}\times\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{emb}_{\mathfrak{B}}}
\textstyle{\sum_{\mathbf{j}\geq{\mathbf{0}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma}
\textstyle{\sum_{\mathbf{j}\geq{\mathbf{0}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}}

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
(
3.11
)
.
∎

There are another two types of linear operators acting on formal series, besides slash operators, which are important in investigating the Taylor expansion of Jacobi forms. The first is the following:

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
. The operator
\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{p}}
is defined on
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
that sends
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}
to
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{p}}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Use notations in Definition
3.1
and put
\gamma=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\right)
. We have

\left(\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{p}}\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\right)|_{k-s(\mathbf{p}),B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma=\mathcal{T}^{\mathbf{p}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma\right).

Proof.

A straightforward calculation.
∎

The second one is the following. Note that
E_{i,j}
denotes the matrix whose
(i,j)
-entry is
1
but all other entries are
0
.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
and
G_{\mathfrak{B}}
be as in Definition
3.1
and let
a_{i,j}
be the
(i,j)
-entry of
G_{\mathfrak{B}}
. The linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}
is defined by
\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}=\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{g}_{\mathbf{p}}T^{\mathbf{p}}
, where

g_{\mathbf{p}}=s(\mathbf{p})(s(\mathbf{p})+2k-2)h_{\mathbf{p}}-4\uppi\sqrt{-1}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq\mathfrak{n}}a_{i,j}\mathop{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}{h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(E_{i,j})}}.

This is a linear operator on
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
. Sometimes we write
\mathcal{L}_{k}
instead of
\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}
when
B
and
\mathfrak{B}
are implicitly known.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
, and
\gamma\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}
. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be as in Definition
3.1
. Then we have

(3.14)

\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma\right)=\left(\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\right)|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma.

Proof.

Put
\gamma=\left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right),{\varepsilon}\right)
. Then the coefficient of
T^{\mathbf{p}}
-term in the left-hand side of (
3.14
) is

\sum_{\Lambda}\frac{(-\uppi\sqrt{-1}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}(c\tau+d)^{-(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda))}\cdot\Big(\\
s(\mathbf{p})(s(\mathbf{p})+2k-2)h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)\\
-4\uppi\sqrt{-1}\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}\cdot(-c)(k+s(\mathbf{p})-2-s(\Lambda))(c\tau+d)h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(E_{i,j})-\pi(\Lambda)}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)\\
-4\uppi\sqrt{-1}\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}\cdot\left(\mathop{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(E_{i,j})-\pi(\Lambda)}\right)\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)\Big).

On the other hand, the coefficient of
T^{\mathbf{p}}
-term in the right-hand side of (
3.14
) is

\sum_{\Lambda}\frac{(-\uppi\sqrt{-1}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}(c\tau+d)^{-(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda))}\cdot\Big(\\
s(\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda))(s(\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda))+2k-2)h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)\\
-4\uppi\sqrt{-1}\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}\left(\mathop{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)-\pi(E_{i,j})}\right)\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)\Big).

Hence (
3.14
) is equivalent to

(3.15)

\sum_{\Lambda}F_{\Lambda}\cdot(s(\mathbf{p})(s(\mathbf{p})+2k-2)-s(\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda))(s(\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda))+2k-2))h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda)}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)\\
=\sum_{\Lambda}F_{\Lambda}\cdot 4\uppi\sqrt{-1}\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}(-c)(k+s(\mathbf{p})-2-s(\Lambda))(c\tau+d)h_{\mathbf{p}-\pi(E_{i,j})-\pi(\Lambda)}\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right),

where

F_{\Lambda}=\frac{(-\uppi\sqrt{-1}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}(c\tau+d)^{-(k+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda))}.

This formula can be proved, using the fact

s(\mathbf{p})(s(\mathbf{p})+2k-2)-s(\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda))(s(\mathbf{p}-\pi(\Lambda))+2k-2)=4s(\Lambda)(k-1+s(\mathbf{p})-s(\Lambda)),

and

4\frac{(-\uppi\sqrt{-1}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda}}{\Lambda!}s(\Lambda)=4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{1\leq i,j\leq\mathfrak{n}}\\
{\lambda_{i,j}>0}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-\uppi\sqrt{-1}cG_{\mathfrak{B}})^{\Lambda-E_{i,j}}}{(\Lambda-E_{i,j})!}(-\uppi\sqrt{-1}c\cdot a_{i,j}).

Here
\lambda_{i,j}
means the
(i,j)
-entry of
\Lambda
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

The composition
\mathcal{T}^{-\mathbf{p}}\circ\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}
is of particular interest when
s(\mathbf{p})=2
. For one-dimensional case, that is, the case
\mathfrak{n}=1
(in this case
\mathbf{p}
is the integer
2
), this operator maps
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{n}T_{1}^{n}
to
\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}((n+2)(n+2k)h_{n+2}-8\uppi\sqrt{-1}m\mathop{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}h_{n})T_{1}^{n}
, where we write
G_{\mathfrak{B}}=(2m)
. This is the modified heat operator introduced in the proof of
[
EZ85
, Theorem 3.2]
, but differs by a factor
-1
.

After defining necessary operators and exploring their basic properties, we now introduce the main object in this section.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, and
\rho\colon\widetilde{H}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(\mathcal{W})
a representation. Fix an ordered
\mathbb{R}
-basis
\mathfrak{B}
of
V
. Let
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}
be a formal series in
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\gg-\infty}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}
. We say that
\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}
transforms like a modular form under the group
H
of weight
k
with representation
\rho
, if

\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}|_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}\gamma=\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{\rho(\gamma)\circ h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}

for any
\gamma\in\widetilde{H}
. The
\mathbb{C}
-space of all such series is denoted by
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(H,\rho)
.

Remark 3.13
.

The relationship between Jacobi forms of lattice index and formal sereis which transform like modular forms are as follows. Recall notations in Convention
1
and
2
, and recall
\mathop{emb}_{\mathfrak{B}}
used in Proposition
3.6
. Then the image of
J_{k,\underline{L}}^{\mathcal{O}}(G,\rho)
under
\mathop{emb}_{\mathfrak{B}}
is contained in
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(G,\rho|_{\widetilde{G}})
, which is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.6
.

Some subspaces of
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(H,\rho)
are also necessary for us. We define

(3.16)

\displaystyle M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}}}}(H,\rho)

\displaystyle=M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(H,\rho)\cap\sum_{\mathbf{j}\geq{\mathbf{j_{0}}}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{H},\mathcal{W})T^{\mathbf{j}}

(3.17)

\displaystyle M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}},+}}(H,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}}}}(H,\rho)\colon h_{\mathbf{j}}\neq 0\implies 2\mid s(\mathbf{j})\}

(3.18)

\displaystyle M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}},-}}(H,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}}}}(H,\rho)\colon h_{\mathbf{j}}\neq 0\implies 2\nmid s(\mathbf{j})\}.

The following are another subspaces, in which
s_{0}
is any integer:

(3.19)

\displaystyle M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0}}}(H,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(H,\rho)\colon h_{\mathbf{j}}\neq 0\implies s(\mathbf{j})\geq s_{0}\}

(3.20)

\displaystyle M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0},+}}(H,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0}}}(H,\rho)\colon h_{\mathbf{j}}\neq 0\implies 2\mid s(\mathbf{j})\}

(3.21)

\displaystyle M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0},-}}(H,\rho)

\displaystyle=\{\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}}{h}_{\mathbf{j}}T^{\mathbf{j}}\in M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0}}}(H,\rho)\colon h_{\mathbf{j}}\neq 0\implies 2\nmid s(\mathbf{j})\}.

It is immediate that
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}}}}(H,\rho)=M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}},+}}(H,\rho)\oplus M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}},-}}(H,\rho)
and
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0}}}(H,\rho)=M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0},+}}(H,\rho)\oplus M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0},-}}(H,\rho)
. We call (
3.17
) and (
3.20
) plus spaces, (
3.18
) and (
3.21
) minus spaces.

Now we investigate how the operators
\mathcal{T}^{-\mathbf{p}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}
act on above spaces.

Proposition 3.14
.

Use notations in Definition
3.12
. Let
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{j_{0}}
be two vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
, and let
s_{0}
be an integer. Then
\mathcal{T}^{-\mathbf{p}}
maps
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(H,\rho)
,
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}}}}(H,\rho)
, and
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0}}}(H,\rho)
, bijectively onto
M_{k+s(\mathbf{p}),B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(H,\rho)
,
M_{k+s(\mathbf{p}),B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{\mathbf{j_{0}}-\mathbf{p}}}(H,\rho)
, and
M_{k+s(\mathbf{p}),B}^{\mathfrak{B},\,{s_{0}-s(\mathbf{p})}}(H,\rho)
respectively. Moreover, if
2\mid s(\mathbf{p})
, then
\mathcal{T}^{-\mathbf{p}}
maps plus (minus resp.) spaces bijectively onto corresponding plus (minus resp.) spaces. If
2\nmid s(\mathbf{p})
, then
\mathcal{T}^{-\mathbf{p}}
maps plus (minus resp.) spaces bijectively onto corresponding minus (plus resp.) spaces.

Proof.

It’s a consequence of Proposition
3.8
.
∎

Proposition 3.15
.

Use notations in Definition
3.12
. Let
\mathbf{j_{0}}
be a vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{\mathfrak{n}}
, and let
s_{0}
be an integer. Then
\mathcal{L}_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}
maps
M_{k,B}^{\mathfrak{B}}(H,\rho)
,
